          "text": "## 2.2.2. Dissolution of LiCoO2 in mixture of glycine and ascorbic acid\nThe above cathode material (LiCoO2, 0.2: g) was subjected for chemical dissolution using mild organic acids, an aqueous mixture of glycine and ascorbic acid were taken in a three necked roundbottomed flask with volume of100 mL and the flask was placed in a water bath to control the reaction temperature during the leaching. The flask was fitted with vapor condenser and a magnetic stirrer. In the leaching process, the concentration of tartaric acid was varied (0.1–0.5 M) by keeping ascorbic acid concentration constant (0.02 M). After 6 h leaching at 80 ^∘𝖢,, the solution and insoluble residue were separated by filtration by using (0.2 mm) membrane filter paper and washed with distilled water, yielding a pink filtrate and a black residue.",

          "text": "## 3.2. Effect of glycine concentration on leaching\nThe effect of glycine concentration on the leaching efficiency of waste LiCoO2 have been studied by varying concentration from\n0.1 M to 0.5 M at temperature of 80 ^∘C for 6 h. The ascorbic acid (reductant) concentration used is 0.02 M and was kept constant. Fig. 4 shows the concentration dependency for leaching cobalt. The leaching efficiency of Co increases from 70% to 95% as the glycine concentration increases from 0.1 to 0.5 M. Therefore, here we used 0.5 M glycine with 0.02 M ascorbic acid for further leaching studies.\nThere are many studies on organic acid leaching. They have obtained more than 90% leaching of Co and about 100% leaching of Li in about 90–120 min by using 1.25 M citric acid [30], 1.5 M DL-malic acid [19], 1.0 M oxalic acid [31] along with H O as reducing agent. In all these studies they have used more concentrations to dissolve the metal ions completely, but in our case as reported in our previous study we have used mild concentrations; 0.1 M citric acid gives 80%, 0.4 M tartaric acid gives more than 90%, 0.1 M iminodiacetic acid gives 91% and 0.1 M maleic acid gives 97% Co and nearly 100% Li ions dissolution in the presence of 0.02 M ascorbic acid [20,24,25].",

          "text": "## 3.4. Effect of temperature and time on the leaching\nThe energy consumption of leaching will be a function of the working temperature and time. The effect of temperature and time on the leaching efficiency was studied by fixing the concentration of ascorbic acid (0.02 M) with glycine (0.1–0.5 M). There are many studies have shown the better leaching capacity for about 80 - 90 ^∘𝖢,, on the basis of these studies we have carried out leaching at 80 \u0001C [15,21,31]. Higher reaction temperature enhances the metal leaching, is due to the endothermicity of leachant dissociation, which translates to greater acid dissolution and increased reaction rates at elevated temperatures [19]. Above 90 ^∘ C, recovery of metal ions decreases because the acids begin to volatilize and the leachant solution becomes less.",

          "text": "## 3.6. Recovery of cobalt as cobalt oxalate\nAfter 6 h of dissolution, on adding oxalic acid to the filtrate, Co (II)–glycine ions were precipitated as Co(II)–oxalate at room temperature immediately. The solid Co(II)–oxalate formed here was separated easily by filtration and washed with excess deionized water, dried in an oven. There was no Co remained in the filtrate. Therefore, a complete recovery of Co is achieved here. Fig. 7 shows the XRD pattern and Fig. 8 shows the FTIR spectrum of this solid powder sample. The XRD pattern of solid residues was confirmed to be well crystallized orthorhombic structured Co(II)– oxalate with space group of Cccm (JCPDS file: 25-0250) [15,20,31]. The FTIR band approximately3340 cm ^-1 has been assigned to O H stretching vibration and the band approximately1710 cm ^-1 has been assigned to CO stretching. The two peaks around 1310 cm\u00051 and 1200 cm ^-1 are due to the presence of carbonyl group. The peak around 966 cm ^-1 could be ascribed to double bond stretching vibration of recovered compounds. The spectrum indicates O H group of the molecular water and the carbonyl group double bond group present in Co(II)– oxalate. These values are similar to the previous reports [20,21]. This cobalt oxalate be used as a Co precursor in preparing the LiCoO2 cathode material",
